The coefficient source is the original globalization note and the full lattice extension \cite{globalization,support}. The complete construction and proofs follow.
\subsection{The original semiring and its exact topology}

Let $L$ be a finite nontrivial bounded distributive lattice. Its bottom
and top are $0_L$ and $1_L$. For a nonzero ring $R$, retain the semiring
\[
 \G_L(R)=\{z_\lambda=(0,\lambda):\lambda\in L\}
     \cup\{\widehat a=(a,1_L):a\in R\}\subset R\times L.
 \tag{FL1}\label{FL:eq:semiring}
\]
The overlap is $e=z_{1_L}=\widehat0$, and the additive identity is
$\tau=z_{0_L}\ne e$. Addition is coefficient addition and lattice
join; multiplication is coefficient multiplication and lattice meet.
In particular
\[
 z_\lambda+z_\mu=z_{\lambda\vee\mu},\quad
 z_\lambda z_\mu=z_{\lambda\wedge\mu},\quad
 \widehat a+z_\lambda=\widehat a,\quad
 \widehat a z_\lambda=z_\lambda.
 \tag{FL2}\label{FL:eq:operations}
\]
Every coefficient and every support label remains present.

\begin{proposition}[All strata and all opens]\label{FL:prop:ordinal-spectrum}
Put $S=\SpecLat L$ and $X=\Spec R$. The prime spectrum of
$\G_L(R)$ is the disjoint union $Y=S\sqcup X$ with primes
\[
 P_{\mathfrak a}=\{z_\lambda:\lambda\in\mathfrak a\},\qquad
 Q_{\mathfrak p}=\{z_\lambda:\lambda\in L\}
                   \cup\{\widehat a:a\in\mathfrak p\}.
 \tag{FL3}\label{FL:eq:all-primes}
\]
Here $\mathfrak a$ is a prime lattice ideal and $\mathfrak p$ a ring
prime. The order in either stratum is its original prime-inclusion
order, and $P_{\mathfrak a}\subsetneq Q_{\mathfrak p}$ for every pair.
The opens of $Y$ are exactly the opens of $S$ and the sets
$S\cup U$ with nonempty open $U\subset X$. In particular
$D(e)=S$ and $V(e)=X$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An ideal containing a supported element $\widehat a$ contains $e$
because $\widehat a e=e$, and then contains every $z_\lambda$ because
$ez_\lambda=z_\lambda$. Its supported coefficients form a ring ideal:
closure under negatives is multiplication by $\widehat{-1}$. Its
primality is exactly primality of that ring ideal. An ideal with no
supported element consists of zero-coefficient labels from a proper
lattice ideal. Addition gives join closure and multiplication by
$z_\mu$ for $\mu\le\lambda$ gives downward closure. Its primality is
exactly the meet-prime condition. Conversely these constructions give
ideals and verify the corresponding primality conditions, proving
\eqref{FL:eq:all-primes}. A pure support prime omits $e$ whereas every
arithmetic prime contains the whole zero-coefficient lattice. This
proves every stated inclusion. Finally the basic opens are
\[
 D(z_\lambda)=\{P_{\mathfrak a}:\lambda\notin\mathfrak a\},\qquad
 D(\widehat a)=S\cup D_R(a).
 \tag{FL4}\label{FL:eq:basic-opens}
\]
Unions of these give exactly the asserted topology and both stratum
identifications.
\end{proof}

For completeness, the support spectrum here is nonempty and $T_0$.
Choose a maximal proper lattice ideal $I$, possible because $L$ is
finite and nontrivial. If $a,b\notin I$ but $a\wedge b\in I$,
maximality gives $u\vee a=1_L$ and $v\vee b=1_L$ for some $u,v\in I$.
Distributivity expands $(u\vee a)\wedge(v\vee b)$ as a join of four
elements of $I$, including $a\wedge b$, a contradiction. Thus $I$
is prime. Distinct prime ideals differ on some label $\lambda$, and
the basic open $D(z_\lambda)$ separates them, proving $T_0$.

More generally, take any nonempty finite $T_0$ space $S$ and any
nonempty irreducible space $X$ and give $Y=S\triangleright X$ the
same topology. Write $j:S\hookrightarrow Y$ for the open inclusion
and $i:X\hookrightarrow Y$ for the closed inclusion. The applications
below use $X=\Spec\mathbb Z$. No semiring additive-module action is
implicitly imposed on the abelian sheaves: the receiving category
throughout is the category of abelian sheaves on this whole topology.

We use the specialization convention that $s\le t$ means that $s$
generalizes $t$. Thus opens in a finite $T_0$ space are lower sets,
the smallest neighbourhood of $s$ is $\mathord\downarrow s$, and a
sheaf has a restriction map from its stalk at $t$ to its stalk at $s$
when $s\le t$. This fixes all cochain orientations below.

\subsection{Sheaves, universal coupling, and derived support}

Let $c_X B$ denote the constant sheaf on the irreducible space $X$:
its value on every nonempty open is $B$ and every such restriction is
the identity. Let $c_S B$ denote the usual constant sheaf on $S$;
its value on an open $V\subset S$ is one copy of $B$ for each connected
component of $V$. For an abelian sheaf $\mathcal A$ on $X$ define
\[
 g\mathcal A=\varinjlim_{\varnothing\ne U\subset X}\mathcal A(U).
 \tag{FL5}\label{FL:eq:generic}
\]
The nonempty opens form a filtered restriction system because $X$ is
irreducible. The functor $g$ is exact. Indeed a sheaf epimorphism lifts
a section on some nonempty member of an open cover, which suffices for
its generic germ, and a generic germ with zero image becomes zero on
a smaller nonempty open, where the kernel sheaf gives exactness.
There are adjunctions
\[
 g\dashv c_X,\qquad c_S\dashv\Gamma(S,-).
 \tag{FL6}\label{FL:eq:constant-adjunctions}
\]
The first expresses that compatible maps $\mathcal A(U)\to B$ are
exactly maps from their colimit; the second follows because a map from
constant sections is determined by their global images and then
sheafification. Both constant-sheaf functors are exact, as is seen on
stalks. In particular $c_X$ preserves injectives, and applying it to
an injective resolution of $B$ proves
\[
 R\Gamma(X,c_XB)=B[0].
 \tag{FL7}\label{FL:eq:constant-acyclic}
\]

\begin{theorem}[Full gluing over a finite support space]
\label{FL:thm:finite-gluing}
An abelian sheaf on $Y$ is exactly a triple
\[
 \mathcal F=(\mathcal A,\mathcal B,\phi),\qquad
 \phi:\mathcal A\longrightarrow c_X\Gamma(S,\mathcal B),
 \tag{FL8}\label{FL:eq:triple}
\]
with $\mathcal A$ a sheaf on $X$ and $\mathcal B$ a sheaf on $S$.
Its sections on $S\cup U$ are $\mathcal A(U)$ and its sections on an
open $V\subset S$ are $\mathcal B(V)$. Restriction from $S\cup U$ to
$V$ is $\phi_U$ followed by restriction from $S$ to $V$.
A morphism $(a,b)$ satisfies
$\phi'a=c_X\Gamma(S,b)\phi$. Exactness is detected on the two
components. The elementary functors are
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
 i^*\mathcal F=\mathcal A,&i_*\mathcal A=(\mathcal A,0,0),&
 i^!\mathcal F=\ker\phi,\\
 j^*\mathcal F=\mathcal B,&j_!\mathcal B=(0,\mathcal B,0),&
 j_*\mathcal B=(c_X\Gamma(S,\mathcal B),\mathcal B,\mathrm{id}).
\end{array}
 \tag{FL9}\label{FL:eq:functors}
\]
Moreover the exact left adjoint of $i^*$ is
\[
 U_S\mathcal A=
 (\mathcal A,c_Sg\mathcal A,\epsilon_S),
 \tag{FL10}\label{FL:eq:universal}
\]
where $\epsilon_S$ sends a section to the constant support section
with its original generic germ. This is the universal coupling to
the entire support space.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Starting with a sheaf on $Y$, set
$\mathcal A(U)=\mathcal F(S\cup U)$ for nonempty $U$ and
$\mathcal B=j^*\mathcal F$. Any pair of nonempty arithmetic opens
intersects. Consequently the sheaf gluing condition for the opens
$S\cup U_a$ is exactly that for the corresponding opens $U_a$ of $X$.
These values therefore form a sheaf $\mathcal A$. Restriction to all
of $S$ gives $\phi$. The mixed open $S\cup U$ is the smallest open
of $Y$ containing $U$, so the resulting sheaf is precisely $i^*\mathcal F$.

Conversely a triple gives the displayed restrictions. A cover of a
mixed open has mixed members covering its arithmetic part. Sections
on those members glue in $\mathcal A$; compatibility with any member
lying wholly in $S$ is exactly its prescribed restriction from
$\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)$. Covers lying wholly in $S$ use the sheaf
axiom for $\mathcal B$. This proves the sheaf axiom and the inverse
classification. The morphism equation follows from restriction to
$S$. Kernels and cokernels are computed on the two strata: they are
the inverse-image sheaves on $X$ and $S$, and stalkwise exactness on
these disjoint strata is exactness on $Y$. The induced cokernel arrow
is obtained by composing to $\Gamma(S,\coker b)$; no exactness of
$\Gamma(S,-)$ is being assumed.

The formulas for $i^*,i_*,i^!,j^*,j_!,j_*$ follow by the Hom
adjunctions: a map from $i_*\mathcal C$ lands in $\ker\phi$, and a
map to $j_*\mathcal D$ has its arithmetic part forced by its support
part. The putative $j_!$ has zero sections on every mixed open. This
is a sheaf because a cover of a mixed open has a mixed member whose
intersection with $S$ is all of $S$, forcing any compatible support
section to vanish. It has the required left adjunction to $j^*$.

For the final adjunction, a map $U_S\mathcal C\to\mathcal F$ with
arithmetic part $a$ has its support map uniquely determined by the
composite $\mathcal C\xrightarrow a\mathcal A\xrightarrow\phi
c_X\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)$: apply successively the two adjunctions in
\eqref{FL:eq:constant-adjunctions}. Conversely that map satisfies the
gluing equation. Thus $U_S\dashv i^*$. Exactness of $U_S$ follows
from exactness of $g,c_S$, and the componentwise exactness already
proved. All assertions follow without dropping a support point.
\end{proof}

The distinction from a single open point is that $\Gamma(S,-)$, and
hence $j_*$, can have higher derived functors. They must be retained.
We use cohomological degrees, so $B[-1]$ has $B$ in degree one.

\begin{theorem}[Derived gluing including support higher cohomology]
\label{FL:thm:derived-finite}
For $\mathcal F=(\mathcal A,\mathcal B,\phi)$ there are natural formulas
\[
 Ri^!\mathcal F\simeq
 \operatorname{Cone}\bigl(\mathcal A\longrightarrow
          c_XR\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)\bigr)[-1],
 \tag{FL11}\label{FL:eq:derived-closed}
\]
\[
 R\Gamma(Y,\mathcal F)=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal A),\qquad
 R\Gamma_X(Y,\mathcal F)\simeq
 \operatorname{Cone}\bigl(R\Gamma(X,\mathcal A)
                \longrightarrow R\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)\bigr)[-1].
 \tag{FL12}\label{FL:eq:derived-global}
\]
The maps are induced by the actual restriction to $S$ in
\eqref{FL:eq:triple}. The localization triangle is
\[
 i_*Ri^!\mathcal F\longrightarrow\mathcal F
 \longrightarrow Rj_*\mathcal B\longrightarrow i_*Ri^!\mathcal F[1].
 \tag{FL13}\label{FL:eq:localization}
\]
Here $R\Gamma_X(Y,-)$ denotes derived sections supported in the closed
arithmetic stratum. The formula does not replace $R\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)$
by its zeroth cohomology.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The functors $i^*$ and $j^*$ are exact. Their right adjoints $i_*$ and
$j_*$ preserve injectives. Also $i^*$ preserves injectives because
its left adjoint $U_S$ is exact, and $j^*$ preserves injectives because
$j_!$ is exact.
If $\mathcal I$ is injective on $Y$, write
$\mathcal B_I=j^*\mathcal I$. Extend the identity support map
$j_!\mathcal B_I\to\mathcal I$ along the monomorphism
$j_!\mathcal B_I\to j_*\mathcal B_I$. Injectivity supplies a map
$j_*\mathcal B_I\to\mathcal I$ right inverse to the restriction
$\mathcal I\to j_*\mathcal B_I$: on $S$ its composite is the identity,
and on $X$ the gluing equation forces the identity on
$c_X\Gamma(S,\mathcal B_I)$. Thus
\[
 0\to i_*i^!\mathcal I\to\mathcal I\to j_*j^*\mathcal I\to0
\]
is split exact. Apply this to an injective resolution of $\mathcal F$.
Its support restriction is an injective resolution of $\mathcal B$;
the corresponding $j_*$ complex computes $Rj_*\mathcal B$.
The kernel complex computes $i_*Ri^!\mathcal F$, proving
\eqref{FL:eq:localization}. Restriction to $X$ turns the $j_*$ complex
into $c_X\Gamma(S,j^*\mathcal I^\bullet)$, which is exactly
$c_XR\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)$. This proves
\eqref{FL:eq:derived-closed}. Global sections on $Y$ are the sections
of $\mathcal A$ on $X$, and $i^*$ preserves injectives, proving the
first formula in \eqref{FL:eq:derived-global}. Applying global sections
to \eqref{FL:eq:derived-closed} and using
\eqref{FL:eq:constant-acyclic} proves the second. At degree zero the
kernel consists precisely of sections restricting to zero on the
open stratum, which verifies the asserted support interpretation.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Finite cochains with their complete orientation}

For the next calculation, identify a finite $T_0$ space with its poset
under the convention above. A sheaf $\mathcal B$ is equivalently the
diagram of stalks $B_s=\mathcal B(\mathord\downarrow s)$ and restriction
maps $r_{t,s}:B_t\to B_s$ for $s\le t$. Indeed a section on a lower
open is a compatible family of these germs, since the smallest
neighbourhoods cover it, and sheaf gluing recovers exactly such families.

\begin{theorem}[The full finite cochain resolution]\label{FL:thm:finite-cochains}
For a sheaf of rational vector spaces on a finite $T_0$ space $S$,
$R\Gamma(S,\mathcal B)$ is represented by
\[
 C^n(S,\mathcal B)=\prod_{s_0<\cdots<s_n}B_{s_0},
 \tag{FL14}\label{FL:eq:cochains}
\]
with differential
\[
 (dc)_{s_0<\cdots<s_{n+1}}
 =r_{s_1,s_0}c_{s_1<\cdots<s_{n+1}}
   +\sum_{a=1}^{n+1}(-1)^a
       c_{s_0<\cdots<\widehat{s_a}<\cdots<s_{n+1}}.
 \tag{FL15}\label{FL:eq:cochain-differential}
\]
For constant coefficients $\RQ$ every restriction is the identity,
so the degree-zero differential is
$(db)_{s<t}=b_t-b_s$. No chain with a repeated point is included.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Computing with rational sheaves gives the same derived sections as
computing with their underlying abelian sheaves. Indeed the forgetful
functor is right adjoint to sheafwise rationalization
$\RQ\otimes_{\mathbb Z}-$; rationalization is exact because it is
localization of abelian groups. Thus an injective rational sheaf
remains injective after forgetting its rational scalar action.
For $t\in S$ and a rational vector space $M$, let $I_t(M)$ be the
sheaf with value $M$ on an open containing $t$ and zero otherwise.
This is direct image from the one-point subspace. Its stalk at $q$
is $M$ if $t\le q$ and zero otherwise. It is right adjoint to the
exact stalk functor at $t$. Since every rational vector space is
injective, $I_t(M)$ is an injective sheaf.

Define the sheaf
\[
 \mathcal C^n=\prod_{s_0<\cdots<s_n}I_{s_n}(B_{s_0}).
\]
Its value on a lower open $V$ is the product over all chains contained
in $V$, since having $s_n\in V$ forces every preceding point into
$V$. The formula \eqref{FL:eq:cochain-differential} therefore defines a
sheaf differential. Expanding $d^2$ cancels every twice-deleted face
with the opposite order of deletion; the two terms using a restriction
cancel because $r_{s_2,s_0}=r_{s_1,s_0}r_{s_2,s_1}$.
The augmentation $\mathcal B\to\mathcal C^0$ is the family of germs.

At a stalk $q$, the augmented complex is that on
$\mathord\downarrow q$, which has maximal point $q$.
Its contracting map from degree zero to the augmentation is evaluation
at $q$. For $n\ge1$, its contracting map from degree $n$ to degree
$n-1$ is
\[
 (hc)_{s_0<\cdots<s_{n-1}}=
 \begin{cases}
 (-1)^n c_{s_0<\cdots<s_{n-1}<q},&s_{n-1}<q,\\
 0,&s_{n-1}=q.
 \end{cases}
\]
The identity $dh+hd=1$ follows by pairing the faces omitting one of
$s_0,\ldots,s_{n-1}$, which cancel, leaving only the face omitting
$q$. In degree zero the additional term is the restriction of the
value at $q$, so that identity includes the augmentation. This proves
stalkwise exactness of the augmented complex. The sheaves
$\mathcal C^n$ are finite products of injectives and vanish above the
maximum strict-chain length. They give an injective resolution, whose
global sections are precisely \eqref{FL:eq:cochains}. This proves the
claimed derived computation with all its signs.
\end{proof}

For an order-preserving map $f:S'\to S$, the induced pullback on
constant-coefficient cochains is
\[
 (f^*c)_{t_0<\cdots<t_n}=
 \begin{cases}
 c_{f(t_0)<\cdots<f(t_n)},&f(t_0),\ldots,f(t_n)\text{ are distinct},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \tag{FL16}\label{FL:eq:cochain-map}
\]
At degree zero it is the usual coordinate pullback. This commutes
with $d$. If the image chain has no repetitions it is immediate from
\eqref{FL:eq:cochain-differential}. If it has a repetition, terms deleting
either member of an adjacent equal pair have the same value and
opposite signs; all other terms still have a repetition and are zero.
This proves the identity in that case as well. The construction is
functorial and gives the map induced by restriction on the injective
cochain resolutions and their augmentations. More explicitly, at a
point $t\in S'$ it pulls cochains on $\mathord\downarrow f(t)$ back
to cochains on $\mathord\downarrow t$, by the displayed formula.
These maps commute with restriction to smaller minimal opens, so
define a sheaf cochain map from the inverse-image resolution to the
resolution on $S'$. Its augmentation is the identity on constant
coefficients. This identifies its global cochain map with the actual
derived pullback. Equivalently it is the
map on the cochains of strict chains after removing repeated chains;
the cancellation just proved verifies that removal directly.

\subsection{The universal arithmetic sheaf for every finite support}

Take $X=\Spec\mathbb Z$, so its generic stalk is $\RQ$. Define the
actual sheaf on the whole ordinal-sum space
\[
 \mathcal U_S=U_S\mathcal O_X
   =(\mathcal O_X,\RQ_S,\text{generic constant restriction}).
 \tag{FL17}\label{FL:eq:arithmetic-object}
\]
The morphism sends each original regular integer-valued rational
section to its same rational generic value at every support point.
In particular it is diagonal on disconnected support components.

We will use $R\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)=\mathbb Z[0]$, including its
vanishing in higher degrees. Here is a direct verification in this
case. On a nonempty open $U=D(N)$, regular functions are the rational
numbers whose denominator has prime factors only among those dividing
$N$. The quotient sheaf $\PP_X=\RQ_X/\mathcal O_X$ has
\[
 \PP_X(U)=\bigoplus_{p\in U\text{ closed}}\RQ/\mathbb Z_{(p)}.
 \tag{FL18}\label{FL:eq:principal-parts}
\]
The rational section map onto this direct sum is surjective: represent
each of finitely many prescribed $p$ classes by $a_p/p^{n_p}$ and
sum them; each other summand is integral at that prime. Its kernel
is precisely $\mathcal O_X(U)$. Formula \eqref{FL:eq:principal-parts}
is a sheaf, since opens of this Noetherian space are quasi-compact
and compatible finite-support sections glue with finite support.
Restrictions discard the removed primes and are surjective, so
$\PP_X$ is flasque. The rational constant sheaf is also flasque.
Their exact sequence is therefore an acyclic resolution of
$\mathcal O_X$. The surjectivity just proved at $U=X$ makes its
global-sections complex have only $\mathbb Z$ in degree zero, proving
the claimed calculation. The acyclicity of flasque sheaves follows
from the usual resolution argument: local lifts modulo a flasque
kernel extend and glue by successive extension over open sets, so a
flasque resolution has an exact global-sections complex in positive
degrees. This argument applies to both displayed sheaves.

\begin{theorem}[A complete relative complex for the full support lattice]
\label{FL:thm:whole-relative-complex}
Put $C^\bullet=C^\bullet(S,\RQ)$ with the exact differential
\eqref{FL:eq:cochain-differential}. The relative complex of
$\mathcal U_S$ is represented by
\[
 K_S^0=\mathbb Z,\qquad K_S^n=C^{n-1}\ (n\ge1),\qquad
 d^0(z)=(z)_{s\in S},\quad d^n=d_C^{n-1}\ (n\ge1).
 \tag{FL19}\label{FL:eq:relative-complex}
\]
These signs identify it with the mapping fibre in
\eqref{FL:eq:derived-global}. Explicitly use fibre coordinates
$(a,c)\in A^n\oplus C^{n-1}$ with differential
$(a,c)\mapsto(d_Aa,f(a)-d_Cc)$; this is a model of
$\operatorname{Cone}(f)[-1]$. In support degree $n-1$ multiply this
coordinate by $(-1)^{n-1}$ to obtain the displayed positive
support differential. Let $c$ be the number
of connected components of $S$. Then
\[
 H_X^0(Y,\mathcal U_S)=0,\qquad
 H_X^1(Y,\mathcal U_S)=\RQ^c/\Delta\mathbb Z,
 \qquad
 H_X^n(Y,\mathcal U_S)=H^{n-1}(S,\RQ)\quad(n\ge2).
 \tag{FL20}\label{FL:eq:relative-groups}
\]
On the arithmetic stratum, the entire derived object is
\[
 Ri^!\mathcal U_S\simeq
 [\mathcal O_X\longrightarrow c_XC^0\longrightarrow
 c_XC^1\longrightarrow\cdots],
 \tag{FL21}\label{FL:eq:relative-sheaf-complex}
\]
with $\mathcal O_X$ in degree zero, the displayed generic constant
map in degree zero, and the same support differential thereafter.
In particular
\[
 R^1i^!\mathcal U_S=(\RQ^c)_X/\Delta\mathcal O_X,
 \qquad R^ni^!\mathcal U_S=c_XH^{n-1}(S,\RQ)\quad(n\ge2).
 \tag{FL22}\label{FL:eq:relative-cohomology-sheaves}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:finite-cochains} computes the actual support derived
sections, and \eqref{FL:eq:arithmetic-object} sends a rational number
to its constant zero-cochain. Substitute these maps and
$R\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)=\mathbb Z[0]$ into
Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:derived-finite}. The indicated sign change gives
exactly \eqref{FL:eq:relative-complex} and
\eqref{FL:eq:relative-sheaf-complex}; it does not change their maps on
degree-zero constants.
The kernel of $d_C^0$ consists of functions equal along every
comparable pair, hence constant on each connected component of the
comparability graph. Those graph components are exactly the connected
components of the finite space: a union of graph components is open
and closed, whereas a partition into two open-and-closed sets cannot
separate a comparable pair. Thus that kernel is $\RQ^c$.
The constant integer map is injective since $S$ is nonempty. Taking
cohomology now gives \eqref{FL:eq:relative-groups}. Stalkwise the original
local domain embeds into its generic field, and the same calculation
of the kernel and higher differentials gives
\eqref{FL:eq:relative-cohomology-sheaves}. This proves the derived
formulas and all connecting maps from the actual sheaf data.
\end{proof}

There is a canonical diagonal inclusion of the one-point relative
complex
\[
 [\mathbb Z\hookrightarrow\RQ]\longrightarrow K_S^\bullet,
 \tag{FL23}\label{FL:eq:diagonal-inclusion}
\]
which is the identity on $\mathbb Z$, the all-points constant map
on $\RQ$, and zero in higher degrees. At the sheaf level this is
$[\mathcal O_X\hookrightarrow\RQ_X]\to Ri^!\mathcal U_S$.
Its quotient is the shifted reduced support complex
\[
 \widetilde C^0=C^0/\Delta\RQ,\qquad
 \widetilde C^n=C^n\ (n\ge1),
 \quad\text{placed starting in relative degree one}.
 \tag{FL24}\label{FL:eq:reduced-support-complex}
\]
Thus the original principal parts and the complete remaining support
complex are linked by an explicit short exact sequence of complexes.
For any specified support point $s$, evaluation of $C^0$ at $s$,
zero in positive support degrees, and the identity on $\mathbb Z$
give a retraction of \eqref{FL:eq:diagonal-inclusion}. It is a cochain
map because the target has no positive support terms. This splits
the complexes once that point is chosen. The diagonal inclusion
itself requires no chosen point.

\subsection{Every Boolean branch and every deletion map}

Let $I$ be a nonempty finite set of cardinality $r$ and take
$L=\mathcal P(I)=\mathbb B^r$. Its prime lattice ideals are
$\mathfrak a_b=\{A\subset I:b\notin A\}$ for $b\in I$.
Indeed a meet-prime ideal must omit some atom; it cannot omit two
distinct atoms because their intersection is empty, and any ideal
omitting the atom $\{b\}$ must contain every other atom. Hence these
are all primes. They are incomparable, so $S_I$ is discrete.

\begin{theorem}[The complete Boolean relative object]
\label{FL:thm:boolean-relative}
For $Y_I=S_I\triangleright\Spec\mathbb Z$ the universal arithmetic
coupling has
\[
 Ri^!\mathcal U_I=[\mathcal O_X\xrightarrow\Delta\RQ_X^I],
 \qquad H_X^1(Y_I,\mathcal U_I)=\RQ^I/\Delta\mathbb Z,
 \tag{FL25}\label{FL:eq:boolean-relative}
\]
and all other relative cohomology groups vanish. Its exact canonical
sequence is
\[
 0\longrightarrow\RQ/\mathbb Z
 \xrightarrow{\ d\ }\RQ^I/\Delta\mathbb Z
 \xrightarrow{\ q\ }\RQ^I/\Delta\RQ\longrightarrow0,
 \quad d(a+\mathbb Z)=[(a)_{b\in I}],\quad q([v])=v+\Delta\RQ.
 \tag{FL26}\label{FL:eq:boolean-sequence}
\]
For a chosen branch $b\in I$, the branch map is
$\beta_b([v])=v_b+\mathbb Z$ and a splitting of the sequence is
\[
 v+\Delta\RQ\longmapsto[v-v_b\mathbf1_I].
 \tag{FL27}\label{FL:eq:branch-splitting}
\]
In particular the explicit branch coordinates are
\[
 \RQ^I/\Delta\mathbb Z\xrightarrow{\sim}
 \RQ/\mathbb Z\oplus\RQ^{I\setminus\{b\}},\qquad
 [v]\longmapsto(v_b+\mathbb Z,(v_a-v_b)_{a\ne b}).
 \tag{FL28}\label{FL:eq:branch-coordinates}
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For discrete $S_I$, the support complex is $\RQ^I$ in degree zero
and zero elsewhere. Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:whole-relative-complex} gives
\eqref{FL:eq:boolean-relative}. In \eqref{FL:eq:boolean-sequence}, the
first map is injective because a diagonal vector is an integer
diagonal exactly when its coefficient is an integer. The kernel of
the second consists exactly of rational diagonal vectors modulo
integer diagonals; the second map is surjective. This proves the
sequence. Changing a representative $v$ by a rational diagonal
changes $v_b$ by the same rational, so
$v-v_b\mathbf1_I$ is unchanged. Its class projects to
$v+\Delta\RQ$, proving the splitting. Coordinate evaluation is well
defined modulo integers and is a left inverse of $d$. Finally an
inverse to \eqref{FL:eq:branch-coordinates} sends
$(a+\mathbb Z,(u_c)_{c\ne b})$ to the class of the vector with entry
$a$ at $b$ and entry $a+u_c$ at $c$; changing $a$ by an integer changes
the vector by an integer diagonal. These checks prove every formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Actual branch, deletion, and duplication morphisms]
\label{FL:prop:deletions}
For a map of nonempty finite sets $f:J\to I$, inverse image of subsets
is a bounded lattice homomorphism $\mathcal P(I)\to\mathcal P(J)$.
It induces the original semiring map
$\G_{\mathcal P(I)}(\mathbb Z)\to\G_{\mathcal P(J)}(\mathbb Z)$,
with unchanged supported coefficients, and the corresponding map of
full spectra is
\[
 f_Y:Y_J\to Y_I,\qquad f_Y|_{S_J}=f,\quad f_Y|_X=\mathrm{id}_X.
 \tag{FL29}\label{FL:eq:branch-topology-map}
\]
The pullback of universal arithmetic sheaves is the universal sheaf
on $Y_J$. Its induced relative map is exactly
\[
 \RQ^I/\Delta\mathbb Z\longrightarrow\RQ^J/\Delta\mathbb Z,
 \qquad [v]\longmapsto[(v_{f(b)})_{b\in J}].
 \tag{FL30}\label{FL:eq:branch-cohomology-map}
\]
For a nonempty subset $J\subset I$ this is the deletion projection,
with the exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow\RQ^{I\setminus J}
 \longrightarrow\RQ^I/\Delta\mathbb Z
 \longrightarrow\RQ^J/\Delta\mathbb Z\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{FL31}\label{FL:eq:deletion-kernel}
\]
The first map inserts zeros on $J$. For arbitrary $f$, the kernel
is $\RQ^{I\setminus f(J)}$ by the same insertion, and the cokernel is
$\RQ^J/f^*\RQ^I$; it consists of the differences between duplicated
coordinates in each fibre of $f$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The preimage operation preserves bottom, top, intersections, and
unions, so \eqref{FL:eq:operations} verifies the semiring map. The
inverse image of the lattice prime at $b\in J$ is the lattice prime
at $f(b)$; arithmetic primes contract to themselves. This proves
\eqref{FL:eq:branch-topology-map} on the entire spectrum, not only on
one stratum. A mixed open pulls back to the mixed open with the same
arithmetic part; the constant rational support data pulls back by
the same coordinate map. Thus it identifies the universal sheaves
and gives the map of relative complexes that is the identity on
$\mathbb Z$ and $f^*$ on degree one. This proves
\eqref{FL:eq:branch-cohomology-map}.

For inclusion of $J$, a kernel element restricts to one common integer
on $J$. Subtract its integer diagonal to give zero on $J$, uniquely
because $J$ is nonempty; all remaining coordinates are free rationals.
Surjectivity is extension of a vector by zeros outside $J$. This proves
\eqref{FL:eq:deletion-kernel}. The same reasoning uses $f(J)$ in the
general case. Since $\Delta\mathbb Z\subset f^*\RQ^I$, taking the
quotient of the target gives the stated cokernel. More explicitly it
is $\bigoplus_{a\in f(J)}\RQ^{f^{-1}(a)}/\Delta\RQ$.
\end{proof}

Deleting all support points is a different endpoint: the remaining
space is $X$, its relative complex is $\mathbb Z[0]$, and the map of
complexes from \eqref{FL:eq:boolean-relative} is the identity in degree
zero and zero in degree one. Its relative degree-one target is zero.
The kernel formula \eqref{FL:eq:deletion-kernel} was stated only for
nonempty $J$ and is not extended incorrectly to this endpoint.

\subsection{A finite lattice whose support has a cycle}

Let $P$ have six elements
\[
 v_1,v_2,v_3,e_{12},e_{23},e_{31},
\]
and exactly the strict relations
\[
 v_1<e_{12},\ v_2<e_{12},\ v_2<e_{23},\ v_3<e_{23},\
 v_3<e_{31},\ v_1<e_{31}.
 \tag{FL32}\label{FL:eq:triangle-relations}
\]
These are the vertices and edges of the boundary of a triangle ordered
by face inclusion; there is no filled triangular face. Set
$L=\Down(P)$, with union as join and intersection as meet.

\begin{proposition}[The eighteen-element lattice and its exact spectrum]
\label{FL:prop:triangle-lattice}
The lattice $L$ has exactly eighteen elements. For any finite poset
$P$, the map
\[
 p\longmapsto\mathfrak a_p=\{D\in\Down(P):p\notin D\}
 \tag{FL33}\label{FL:eq:poset-primes}
\]
is a homeomorphism $P\to\SpecLat\Down(P)$ when $P$ has its lower-set
topology. It preserves the stated specialization order: $p\le q$
if and only if $\mathfrak a_p\subseteq\mathfrak a_q$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A downset in the triangle poset first chooses a subset $V$ of its
three vertices and then an arbitrary subset of those edges whose
two endpoints lie in $V$. For $|V|=0,1,2,3$, the numbers of choices
are respectively $1,3,3\cdot2,2^3$. Their sum is
$1+3+6+8=18$.

For a general finite $P$, each displayed $\mathfrak a_p$ is a proper
lattice ideal and is prime: membership in a union is downward-ideal
closure, and if $p$ belongs to both downsets it belongs to their
intersection. To show exhaustion, let $h:\Down(P)\to\{0,1\}$ be the
indicator of the complement of a prime ideal. It preserves union
and intersection by the ideal and prime conditions. Every nonempty
downset is the union of the principal downsets of its members.
Choose a minimal $p$ for which $h(\mathord\downarrow p)=1$; one exists
because $h(P)=1$. If $h(\mathord\downarrow q)=1$, then
$h(\mathord\downarrow p\cap\mathord\downarrow q)=1$. Decomposing that
intersection into principal downsets yields some $r\le p,q$ with
$h(\mathord\downarrow r)=1$. Minimality forces $r=p$, so $p\le q$.
It follows that for every downset $D$, $h(D)=1$ exactly when $p\in D$.
This proves exhaustion and uniqueness. The basic spectral open at a
downset $D$ is exactly $\{\mathfrak a_p:p\in D\}$, which proves the
homeomorphism. Finally if $p\le q$, absence of $p$ from a downset
implies absence of $q$, proving the ideal inclusion. Conversely if
$p\not\le q$, the downset $\mathord\downarrow q$ belongs to
$\mathfrak a_p$ but not $\mathfrak a_q$, disproving inclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Every triangle support cochain and every relative group]
\label{FL:thm:triangle-cohomology}
For $S=\SpecLat L$ with the lattice of
Proposition~\ref{FL:prop:triangle-lattice}, the full constant-rational
support complex is
\[
 C^0=\RQ^6\xrightarrow{D}C^1=\RQ^6,
 \qquad C^n=0\quad(n\ge2),
 \tag{FL34}\label{FL:eq:triangle-complex}
\]
where the degree-zero coordinates are
$(a_1,a_2,a_3,b_{12},b_{23},b_{31})$, the degree-one coordinates are
in exactly the order of \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-relations}, and
\[
 D(a,b)=(b_{12}-a_1,b_{12}-a_2,b_{23}-a_2,
                b_{23}-a_3,b_{31}-a_3,b_{31}-a_1).
 \tag{FL35}\label{FL:eq:triangle-differential}
\]
Its cohomology is $H^0(S,\RQ)=\RQ$ by constant sections and
$H^1(S,\RQ)=\RQ$ by the quotient map
\[
 \omega(c)=c_1-c_2+c_3-c_4+c_5-c_6.
 \tag{FL36}\label{FL:eq:cycle-functional}
\]
The actual relative complex over $\Spec\mathbb Z$ is
\[
 \mathbb Z\xrightarrow{z\mapsto(z,z,z,z,z,z)}\RQ^6
 \xrightarrow{D}\RQ^6
 \quad\text{in degrees }0,1,2.
 \tag{FL37}\label{FL:eq:triangle-relative-complex}
\]
It has precisely
\[
 H_X^1(Y,\mathcal U_S)=\RQ/\mathbb Z,\qquad
 H_X^2(Y,\mathcal U_S)=\RQ,
 \qquad H_X^n(Y,\mathcal U_S)=0\ (n\ne1,2).
 \tag{FL38}\label{FL:eq:triangle-relative-groups}
\]
At the sheaf level $R^1i^!\mathcal U_S=\PP_X$ and
$R^2i^!\mathcal U_S=\RQ_X$, with no other cohomology sheaves.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The six points and the six relations in \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-relations}
are all strict chains of lengths zero and one. There is no strict
chain of length two. Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:finite-cochains} therefore gives
exactly \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-complex} and
\eqref{FL:eq:triangle-differential}. Its kernel forces equal values at
every incident vertex and edge, hence forces all six values equal.
Substitution gives $\omega D=0$.

Conversely let $c\in\RQ^6$ satisfy $\omega(c)=0$. Put
\[
\begin{aligned}
 a_1&=0,& b_{12}&=c_1,& a_2&=c_1-c_2,\\
 b_{23}&=c_1-c_2+c_3,&a_3&=c_1-c_2+c_3-c_4,\\
 b_{31}&=c_1-c_2+c_3-c_4+c_5.
\end{aligned}
 \tag{FL39}\label{FL:eq:triangle-preimage}
\]
The first five equations of $D(a,b)=c$ hold by direct substitution;
the sixth holds because $\omega(c)=0$. Thus $\operatorname{im}D$
is exactly $\ker\omega$. The functional $\omega$ is surjective,
as is seen on $(1,0,0,0,0,0)$, proving the support cohomology and
fixing its generator and orientation.

Insert this complete support complex into
Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:whole-relative-complex}. The resulting relative
differentials are precisely \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-relative-complex}.
Its first cohomology consists of a rational constant six-vector
modulo an integer constant six-vector, identified by $q\mapsto q+\mathbb Z$.
Its second cohomology is the quotient by $D$, identified by
\eqref{FL:eq:cycle-functional}. Its degree-zero kernel is zero, and
there are no further terms. The sheaf-level calculation has the
same support matrix, with the initial diagonal
$\mathcal O_X\hookrightarrow\RQ_X$; its first quotient is
$\RQ_X/\mathcal O_X=\PP_X$, and its second is $\RQ_X$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=1.25]
\node (v1) at (0,2) {$v_1$};
\node (e12) at (1.732,1) {$e_{12}$};
\node (v2) at (1.732,-1) {$v_2$};
\node (e23) at (0,-2) {$e_{23}$};
\node (v3) at (-1.732,-1) {$v_3$};
\node (e31) at (-1.732,1) {$e_{31}$};
\draw[->] (v1)--node[above right] {$c_1$}(e12);
\draw[->] (v2)--node[right] {$c_2$}(e12);
\draw[->] (v2)--node[below right] {$c_3$}(e23);
\draw[->] (v3)--node[below left] {$c_4$}(e23);
\draw[->] (v3)--node[left] {$c_5$}(e31);
\draw[->] (v1)--node[above left] {$c_6$}(e31);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The complete support specialization diagram for the
eighteen-element lattice. Arrows run from a generalizing vertex prime
to a specializing edge prime, so opens are lower sets. The six arrow
coordinates are exactly those of (FL35). The oriented cycle functional
is $c_1-c_2+c_3-c_4+c_5-c_6$; there is no two-simplex filling it.
Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:triangle-cohomology} proves that this contributes
the additional relative group $\RQ$ in degree two. The drawing is an
order diagram and asserts no metric on the prime space.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Maps retaining or deleting the actual cycle}

\begin{proposition}[Support maps and their relative chain maps]
\label{FL:prop:support-morphisms}
For an order-preserving map $f:S'\to S$, the map
$f_Y:S'\triangleright X\to S\triangleright X$ that is $f$ on support
and the identity on $X$ is continuous. Its inverse image takes
$\mathcal U_S$ to $\mathcal U_{S'}$. On relative complexes its map is
the identity on $\mathbb Z$ and the cochain pullback
\eqref{FL:eq:cochain-map} in all support degrees. If the support spaces
are spectra of downset lattices, this is the actual spectral map
induced by the lattice homomorphism $D\mapsto f^{-1}(D)$ and the
coefficient-preserving map of the original semirings.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The preimage of a support open is a support open because $f$ is
order preserving. The preimage of $S\cup U$ is $S'\cup U$, proving
continuity on every open. The arithmetic sheaf is unchanged and
the inverse image of a constant rational sheaf on support is the
constant rational sheaf. The generic-value map stays constant with
the same value, proving the sheaf identification. Its maps on the
relative resolutions are exactly those displayed, by the natural
augmentations, restriction maps, and the cochain computation in
Theorem~\ref{FL:thm:finite-cochains}. The cochain identity was checked
including collapsed chains after \eqref{FL:eq:cochain-map}.
Finally $f^{-1}$ preserves unions and intersections and both bounds.
The preimage of the prime at $s'$, applied to a downset $D$, tests
$s'\notin f^{-1}D$, which is exactly $f(s')\notin D$. Thus its
spectral map is $f$, while supported coefficients are unchanged.
This proves the last assertion on both strata.
\end{proof}

For the triangle, delete the maximal element $e_{31}$ to obtain the
lower open subposet
\[
 T=\{v_1,v_2,v_3,e_{12},e_{23}\}.
\]
Its support cochains are $\RQ^5\to\RQ^4$, with the first four
coordinates of \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-differential}. The explicit
preimage formulas for these four coordinates are the first five
assigned quantities in \eqref{FL:eq:triangle-preimage}; hence that
differential is surjective and its kernel is the rational constants.
The relative groups for $T$ are therefore $\RQ/\mathbb Z$ in degree
one and zero in degree two. The actual inclusion map
$T\triangleright X\to S\triangleright X$ pulls back the relative
complex by deleting $b_{31}$ from degree one and $c_5,c_6$ from
degree two. It induces the identity on the principal-parts
$\RQ/\mathbb Z$ and the zero map from the original cycle $\RQ$.
There is no claim that $T\triangleright X$ is an open subset of the
whole space: it is the indicated continuous subspace embedding,
and the maps are the proved pullbacks of pairs.

The map $S\to\{*\}$ gives a map from the triangle full space to the
one-point full space. Its relative pullback is the canonical diagonal
inclusion \eqref{FL:eq:diagonal-inclusion}. Choosing any point
$*\to S$ gives the evaluation retraction. Thus the ordinary
principal-parts contribution is retained with exact section and
retraction maps, while the new degree-two support cycle is kept in
the kernel of each branch evaluation. These statements specify the
full morphisms, not merely a comparison of dimensions.

\subsection{Scope and proof provenance}

The original coefficient/support definition and its complete prime
classification were read in the existing programme sources
\texttt{01\_spectra.tex}, the theorem
\texttt{thm:rebuilt-full-support}, and
\texttt{08\_full\_stalks.tex}, formulas (TL1)--(TL5). They are
reproved in Proposition~\ref{FL:prop:ordinal-spectrum} to fix the topology
and prevent a change of support object. The earlier independent
\path{split_prime_recollement_pairing.tex}, formulas (RP3)--(RP12)
and (RP21)--(RP25), treated a single extra point and its principal
parts. The present proofs retain the entire finite support and the
higher derived support sections, and do not infer them from that
one-point calculation.

The extra groups in \eqref{FL:eq:relative-groups} and
\eqref{FL:eq:triangle-relative-groups} are actual derived invariants of
the original full-support prime topology and the specified universal
arithmetic coupling. Their receiving category is the abelian-sheaf
category of that topology. They have not been given an incompatible
additive-group semiring-module action. The proofs construct no
Frobenius endomorphism and assign no weight to the new cycle; such
an endomorphism and its trace would have to be computed from actual
maps on the displayed complexes. No Riemann-hypothesis implication
is asserted here.


\section{Full support degree and the original arithmetic quotient}
\label{sec:full-support-degree}
The question addressed here is Deligne's purity condition, with its
actual receiving maps. The supplied English mathematical edition of
Weil II, (3.3.4)--(3.3.6), identifies the image of compactly supported
cohomology in ordinary cohomology as the pure object, after proving
opposite weight bounds and using duality \cite{DeligneII}. We do not
assume an analogous assertion over the split base. We calculate first
what the full support changes in the relative arithmetic group, then
construct an exact map from its prime-degree operator to the original
arithmetic companion action. This includes the attained metric of that
map and its entire distributional extension criterion.

The prime point $\{\tau,e\}$ is retained from the original globalization
paper \cite{globalization}; it is not a discovery of this continuation.
The scalar recollement and prime operators are proved in the preceding
chapters, (RP21)--(RP35), (PD1)--(PD21), and (IA1)--(IA9).
The full finite-support recollement is proved in the accompanying
chapter. The arithmetic matrix is exactly the retained programme
matrix in CLM13--CLM23 of the complete reconstruction source supplied
with this edition. No canonical metric or sheaf-theoretic trace is
inferred from a formal resemblance of operators.

\subsection{The canonical diagonal and all additional support classes}
For a nonempty finite support space $S$, let $c$ be its number of
connected components. The full recollement calculation gives
\[
 D_S=H^1_X(Y,U\mathcal O_X)=\mathbb Q^c/\Delta\mathbb Z,
 \qquad X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z,
 \tag{FD1}
\]
where $\Delta t=(t,\ldots,t)$ has every component present.
The remaining positive relative degrees are
$H_X^{j+1}(Y,U\mathcal O_X)=H^j(S,\mathbb Q)$ for $j\ge1$.
We shall use the complete group in (FD1), without selecting a branch.
There is a canonical exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow D=\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z
 \xrightarrow{\Delta}D_S
 \xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb Q^c/\Delta\mathbb Q
 \longrightarrow0.
 \tag{FD2}
\]
The first map is injective because $\Delta q\in\Delta\mathbb Z$
exactly when $q\in\mathbb Z$. Its image is the kernel of the quotient
map $\pi$. That map is onto by the definition of its codomain. This
proves exactness, including the diagonal lattice, rather than merely
an abstract dimension count. Moreover the torsion subgroup of $D_S$
is exactly $\Delta D$: if $n[x]=0$, then $nx\in\Delta\mathbb Z$,
so $x\in\Delta\mathbb Q$; conversely each diagonal rational class
has finite order. This characterization makes the first term canonical.

For a nonzero integer $n$, multiplication $[x]\mapsto[nx]$ is onto,
since $[x/n]$ is a preimage, and
\[
 \ker(n:D_S\to D_S)
   =\Delta(n^{-1}\mathbb Z)/\Delta\mathbb Z,
 \qquad |\ker n|=|n|.
 \tag{FD3}
\]
Every fibre is a translate of this displayed kernel. On
$D_S/\Delta D$ the same multiplication is an automorphism, because
that quotient is a rational vector space. Multiplication by $n$ on
each $H^j(S,\mathbb Q)$, $j\ge1$, is also an automorphism.
Thus disconnected support adds actual classes but does not replace
the arithmetic degree $|n|$ by $|n|^c$. A cycle in the support adds
higher relative cohomology, whose multiplication-by-$n$ degree is one.

For Boolean support with $c=r$ labelled points, forgetting any set of
coordinates, with at least one coordinate retained, gives the actual
map $D_r\to D_t$ by coordinate projection. Its kernel is isomorphic
to $\mathbb Q^{r-t}$: subtract the common integer from the retained
coordinates of a representative whose image vanishes, leaving those
coordinates zero. The deleted coordinates are then uniquely determined
rationals. These maps commute with (FD3), and are the identity on the
diagonal torsion subgroup. This proves their relation to the scalar
branch, including the entire kernel of every deletion.

\subsection{The Hilbert receiver of the entire relative group}
Use counting measure on the countable group $D_S$, and set
\[
 (A_{n,S}f)(d)=f(nd),\quad
 A_{n,S}^*g(u)=\sum_{nd=u}g(d),\quad
 A_{n,S}^*A_{n,S}=|n|I.
 \tag{FD4}
\]
The formulas first hold on finitely supported functions. Collecting the
sum by fibres in (FD3) proves both the adjoint formula and the exact
norm identity $\|A_{n,S}f\|^2=|n|\|f\|^2$; hence the bounded
extensions and their adjoints obey (FD4). They retain all support
components and every diagonal torsion class.

Extension by zero along $\Delta:D\to D_S$ gives an isometric map
\[
 J_S:\ell^2(D)\hookrightarrow\ell^2(D_S),\qquad
 A_{n,S}J_S=J_S A_n,\quad A_{n,S}^*J_S=J_S A_n^*.
 \tag{FD5}
\]
Indeed $nd$ is torsion if and only if $d$ is torsion, since the quotient
in (FD2) is uniquely divisible and torsion free. The fibre above a
torsion element is consequently entirely in $\Delta D$; the fibre
above a nontorsion element is entirely outside it. These observations
prove both identities point by point. The canonical torsion subspace
and its orthogonal complement are therefore both invariant under the
operator and its adjoint. The original vector $\delta_{0}$ belongs to
this torsion subspace. The original scalar Weil receiver (PD14)--(PD18)
is recovered exactly there; the additional classes have not been
assigned invented coefficients in that scalar receiver.

For a fixed prime $p$, put $V_{p,S}=p^{-1/2}A_{p,S}$. It is an
isometry and is pure in the precise operator-theoretic sense
\[
 \bigcap_{m\ge0}V_{p,S}^m\ell^2(D_S)=\{0\}.
 \tag{FD6}
\]
If $f$ belongs to this intersection, then for every $m$ it is constant
on each coset of $\ker p^m$, because it is in the range of pullback by
$p^m$. Thus it is constant on every coset of the infinite subgroup
$\Delta(\mathbb Z[1/p]/\mathbb Z)=\bigcup_m\ker p^m$.
Square summability forces each such constant to vanish. This proves
(FD6). Here ``pure isometry'' is its stated intersection property;
it is not being identified with Deligne's purity theorem.

For completeness an explicit minimal unitary extension follows without
assuming a dilation theorem. Put $W_S=\ker V_{p,S}^*$. The projections
$V^mV^{*m}-V^{m+1}V^{*(m+1)}$ have mutually orthogonal ranges $V^mW_S$.
Their finite sums telescope to $I-V^{M+1}V^{*(M+1)}$. Decreasing
orthogonal projections converge strongly to the projection onto their
intersection: the squared norm differences telescope, so the projected
vectors are Cauchy, and their limit lies in every range. By (FD6) that
limit is zero. Hence
$\ell^2(D_S)=\bigoplus_{m\ge0}V^mW_S$. On
$\widetilde H_S=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb Z}W_S$ take the bilateral
shift $\widetilde V$ and embed the nonnegative summands as just
identified. The operator
\[
 \widetilde A_{p,S}=\sqrt p\,\widetilde V,
 \qquad \widetilde A_{p,S}^*\widetilde A_{p,S}
        =\widetilde A_{p,S}\widetilde A_{p,S}^*=pI
 \tag{FD7}
\]
extends $A_{p,S}$. The space $W_S$ is nonzero: otherwise $V$ is a
surjective isometry on a nonzero space, contradicting (FD6).
The bilateral shift has spectrum the unit circle and no eigenvectors.
To verify these claims directly, a unit $w\in W_S$ gives orthonormal
translates $w_m$; the unit vectors
$(2M+1)^{-1/2}\sum_{m=-M}^M\lambda^{-m}w_m$ have residual squared
norm $2/(2M+1)$ for $|\lambda|=1$. Neumann series invert the shift
minus $\lambda$ for $|\lambda|\ne1$. An eigenvector on the circle
would have equal component norms at every integer index and is thus
zero. Scaling proves the full circle $|z|=\sqrt p$ for (FD7).
This extends the proved local weight-one norm identity to every class
of the full relative group (FD1).

The Hilbert construction has a precise relation to additive
complexification, which is a different receiver. On finite-support
functions define
\[
 \kappa_S:\mathbb C^{(D_S)}\longrightarrow D_S\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb C
       \simeq\mathbb C^c/\Delta\mathbb C,
 \qquad \kappa_S(\delta_d)=d\otimes1.
 \tag{FD8}
\]
The quotient of the free complex vector space by the relations
$\delta_0=0$ and $\delta_{x+y}-\delta_x-\delta_y=0$ has exactly the
universal property of the displayed tensor product: a complex-linear
map out of it is an additive map from $D_S$ to a complex vector space,
and extends uniquely by $d\otimes z\mapsto z f(d)$. This proves both
the map and its complete kernel presentation. The tensor product kills
every torsion element, because its order is invertible in $\mathbb C$.
Using (FD2), or its explicit coordinates after one branch is specified,
then proves the last isomorphism. In particular $\kappa_S\delta_0=0$
and the entire diagonal torsion subspace is killed.

For every positive integer $n$, this exact receiver satisfies
\[
 \kappa_S A_{n,S}=\kappa_S,\qquad
 \kappa_S A_{n,S}^*=n\kappa_S.
 \tag{FD9}
\]
Indeed $A_{n,S}\delta_u=\sum_{nd=u}\delta_d$. Each of its $n$
preimages has tensor value $(u\otimes1)/n$, since the kernel in
(FD3) is torsion. Their sum is $u\otimes1$, proving the first
identity. The adjoint sends $\delta_d$ to $\delta_{nd}$, which proves
the second. Thus the prime pullback on counting functions, the
degree-$p$ operation on the relative group, and its additive
complexification are linked by specified maps with their exact
factors. They are not silently identified. Retaining the supported
zero and torsion is essential for the original integral vector and
the Weil receiver: they disappear under (FD8), whose kernel has now
been calculated rather than treated as absent data.

The supported integer zero is still present. On the full algebraic
dual of $\mathbb C^{(D_S)}\oplus\mathbb C\delta_\tau$, its pullback is
$(f,c)\mapsto(f(0)\mathbf1_{D_S},c)$, whereas absent $\tau$ gives
$(f,c)\mapsto(c\mathbf1_{D_S},c)$. These distinct maps are proved by
evaluating the two scalar actions, precisely as in (IA7). The first
does not preserve $\ell^2(D_S)$, since $D_S$ is infinite. Its exact
target remains the stated algebraic dual; no integer has been deleted.

\subsection{An explicit quotient onto the original arithmetic action}
Keep the original polynomial, its coordinate, and its arithmetic prime:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 q=(k+1)^2,\quad k\ge17,\quad k\equiv1\pmod4,
 \quad\delta,\gamma>0,\quad p\text{ prime},\\
 Q_k(y)=\prod_{a,b=0}^k[y-(2b-k)\gamma+i(2a-k)\delta],
 \quad E=\mathbb C[y]/(Q_k),\quad M[f]=[yf],\\
 F_p=p^{k/2}\exp(i(\log p)M),\qquad B_p=\exp(i(\log p)M).
 \end{gathered}\tag{AQ1}
\]
The letter $B_p$ denotes this complete centred matrix, not a changed
prime action: $F_p=p^{k/2}B_p$ is retained in every intertwining formula.
At each original cutoff $N\in\{q-1,q,2q-1,2q\}$, let $G_N$ be
the original positive definite metric on $E$ in its original monomial
coordinates. No metric is chosen in its place.

The $k$-fold local relative operator (PD20) acts on
$L^2(\mathbb Q_p^k)$ as $p^k f(x/p)$; its associated unitary operator
is $\mathscr U^{(k)}f(x)=p^{k/2}f(x/p)$. Set
\[
 A=\mathbb Z_p^\times\times\mathbb Z_p^{k-1},\qquad
 h=\mathbf1_A,\qquad h_m=(\mathscr U^{(k)})^mh,
 \quad w_p=\|h_m\|^2=1-p^{-1}.
 \tag{AQ2}
\]
The supports of the $h_m$ are disjoint because the first coordinate
has valuation $m$. Thus their inner products are
$w_p\mathbf1_{m=n}$. The Haar factor $w_p$ is retained, and the
unchanged $k$-fold degree is $p^k$.
Let $\Phi_{p,N}$ be the finite sums $\sum_m h_m\otimes v_m$ in
$L^2(\mathbb Q_p^k)\otimes(E,G_N)$, with the inherited norm. Define
\[
 \Psi_p\Big(\sum_mh_m\otimes v_m\Big)
       =\sum_m B_p^m v_m\in E.
 \tag{AQ3}
\]
Uniqueness of the orthogonal coefficients makes this map well defined;
the sum is finite. It is onto since $h_0\otimes v$ maps to $v$.
For the actual degree operator $T_p=(T_p^{\rm rel})^{\otimes k}\otimes I_E$,
\[
 \Psi_p T_p=F_p\Psi_p,\qquad
 T_p(h_m\otimes v)=p^{k/2}h_{m+1}\otimes v.
 \tag{AQ4}
\]
Both sides of the first identity, on a basis summand, equal
$p^{k/2}B_p^{m+1}v$. This is an exact typed, surjective morphism from
a concrete subspace of the new relative-prime receiver to the original
arithmetic matrix, with its uncentred factor. It does not assert that
the morphism is bounded in the Hilbert norm or is a geometric
compact-to-ordinary cohomology map. Those are mathematical properties
to calculate, rather than inferred identifications.

Its full algebraic kernel is also explicit. Write a finite sequence as
the Laurent polynomial $v(t)=\sum_m t^m v_m$. Then
\[
 \ker\Psi_p=(tI_E-B_p)E[t,t^{-1}].
 \tag{AQ5}
\]
The right side is in the kernel by evaluation. Conversely subtract
$v(B_p)$ from $v(t)$ term by term. For $m>0$,
$t^mI-B_p^m=(tI-B_p)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}t^{m-1-j}B_p^j$.
For $m=-r<0$ use
$t^{-r}I-B_p^{-r}=-t^{-r}B_p^{-r}(t^rI-B_p^r)$.
These identities hold since $t$ commutes with the matrix and $B_p$
is invertible. Their sum proves (AQ5), including all negative powers.
The support-preserving lift of (AQ3)--(AQ5) on synchronized full-$L$
carriers is $(v,\lambda)\mapsto(\Psi_pv,\lambda)$; its layerwise
kernel is $(\ker\Psi_p)^\uparrow_L$. Direct evaluation of addition,
scalar multiplication and (AQ4) proves these assertions, with the
zero map $(v,\lambda)\mapsto(0,\lambda)$ retained. This is the
retraction category and its supported zero of CR15--CR23; it does not
identify that map with the constant-$\tau$ map on a free semimodule.

\subsection{The attained quotient metric, including every cross term}
Restrict (AQ3) to $-J\le m\le J$, for an integer $J\ge0$. Its
source metric is $w_p\operatorname{diag}(G_N,\ldots,G_N)$.
The minimum squared norm among all preimages of $v\in E$ is
\[
 \min_{\sum_{m=-J}^J B_p^mv_m=v}
      w_p\sum_{m=-J}^Jv_m^*G_Nv_m
  =v^*H_{p,J;N}v,
 \quad
 H_{p,J;N}=w_p\left(
   \sum_{m=-J}^J B_p^mG_N^{-1}(B_p^*)^m\right)^{-1}.
 \tag{AQ6}
\]
To prove the minimum, put $C=\sum_mB_p^mG_N^{-1}(B_p^*)^m$.
It is positive definite because the $m=0$ term is. The candidate
$v_m^0=G_N^{-1}(B_p^*)^m C^{-1}v$ has the required sum. For any
other preimage write $v_m=v_m^0+u_m$, so $\sum_mB_p^mu_m=0$.
The cross term in its squared norm is
$2w_p\operatorname{Re}(v^*C^{-1}\sum_mB_p^mu_m)=0$.
The remaining extra term is nonnegative and vanishes only for $u_m=0$.
The candidate's squared norm is $w_pv^*C^{-1}v$, proving (AQ6).
This is a new, explicitly identified attained quotient metric; it has
not been substituted for any older native conductor metric.

All original roots are distinct for $\delta,\gamma>0$. Let
$\mathsf V_{(a,b),j}=y_{ab}^j$ be their original evaluation matrix,
$0\le j<q$, and set
\[
 y_{ab}=(2b-k)\gamma-i(2a-k)\delta,quad
 \alpha_{ab}=p^{(2a-k)\delta}e^{i(2b-k)\gamma\log p},\quad
 D_N=\mathsf V G_N^{-1}\mathsf V^*.
 \tag{AQ7}
\]
Polynomial interpolation proves $\mathsf V$ invertible and
$\mathsf V B_p\mathsf V^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}(\alpha_{ab})$.
Consequently the complete covariance in (AQ6) satisfies
\[
 (\mathsf V C\mathsf V^*)_{ij}
   =(D_N)_{ij}\,\mathcal D_J(\alpha_i\overline{\alpha_j}),
 \quad
 \mathcal D_J(z)=\sum_{m=-J}^J z^m
 =\begin{cases}
 z^{-J}(1-z^{2J+1})/(1-z),&z\ne1,\\
 2J+1,&z=1.
 \end{cases}
 \tag{AQ8}
\]
Matrix multiplication gives each summand, and the finite geometric sum
gives the last expression. It retains every pair of roots, every
phase resonance, and every original metric entry. In particular the
variance of the $i$th original root functional is exactly
$w_p^{-1}(D_N)_{ii}\mathcal D_J(|\alpha_i|^2)$.

For a rigorous size comparison, define the actual constants
$d_{-,N}=\lambda_{\min}(D_N)>0$ and
$d_{+,N}=\lambda_{\max}(D_N)$. Multiplying
$d_-I\preceq D_N\preceq d_+I$ by each diagonal power, summing, and
inverting positive matrices gives
\[
 \frac{w_p}{d_{+,N}}\mathsf V^*
   \operatorname{diag}(\mathcal D_J(|\alpha_i|^2)^{-1})\mathsf V
 \preceq H_{p,J;N}\preceq
 \frac{w_p}{d_{-,N}}\mathsf V^*
   \operatorname{diag}(\mathcal D_J(|\alpha_i|^2)^{-1})\mathsf V.
 \tag{AQ9}
\]
The upper bound is at most
$w_pd_{-,N}^{-1}p^{-2J\delta}\mathsf V^*\mathsf V$, because $k$
is odd and $|2a-k|\ge1$: choose the endpoint $m=J$ or $m=-J$ in
the positive sum to get
$\mathcal D_J(|\alpha_{ab}|^2)\ge p^{2J|2a-k|\delta}$.
This is a proved exponential collapse of this quotient metric for the
displayed off-line family, with all comparison constants specified.
It is not a conclusion about the existence of actual zeta zeros.

For the four original cutoff signs
$\varepsilon_{q-1}=\varepsilon_q=1$ and
$\varepsilon_{2q-1}=\varepsilon_{2q}=-1$, the exact return of this
new metric is
\[
 \sum_N\varepsilon_N\log\det H_{p,J;N}
  =-\sum_N\varepsilon_N\log\det
   \big[(D_N)_{ij}\mathcal D_J(\alpha_i\overline{\alpha_j})\big]_{ij}.
 \tag{AQ10}
\]
Indeed the exact determinant is
\[
 \det H=\frac{w_p^q|\det\mathsf V|^2}{\det(\mathsf V C\mathsf V^*)}.
\]
Both displayed common factors cancel since $\sum_N\varepsilon_N=0$.
The $D_N$ factors do not
cancel by this argument and have all been retained. Formula (AQ10)
is an exact receiving identity, not an asserted asymptotic evaluation.

\subsection{The precise distribution space selected by the quotient}
The original action now determines which sequences it can receive.
For any invertible matrix $B$ on a finite-dimensional metric space,
consider the already defined finite-sequence map
$\Psi_B((v_m))=\sum_m B^mv_m$. Define the sequence space
\[
 \mathscr S(\mathbb Z;E)=
 \{(v_m):\sup_m(1+|m|)^r\|v_m\|_{G_N}<\infty
                   \text{ for every integer }r\ge0\}.
 \tag{AQ11}
\]
Its topology is given by exactly these seminorms. We have the complete
criterion
\[
 \Psi_B\text{ has a continuous extension to }\mathscr S(\mathbb Z;E)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 |\lambda|=1\text{ for every eigenvalue }\lambda\text{ of }B.
 \tag{AQ12}
\]
Here is a proof including nonsemisimple matrices. In a Jordan basis,
a block is $\lambda I+K$, $K^d=0$. For every integer $m$,
\[
 (\lambda I+K)^m
 =\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\binom mj\lambda^{m-j}K^j.
 \tag{AQ13}
\]
For nonnegative $m$ this is the binomial formula. For negative $m$ it
follows by multiplying the finite formal inverse series modulo $K^d$,
or inducting downwards from $m=0$ with that inverse. The coefficients
$\binom mj$ are polynomials in $m$ of degree $j$. If all eigenvalues
have modulus one, the finite Jordan change of basis gives an explicit
constant $C_B$ with $\|B^m\|_{G_N}\le C_B(1+|m|)^{d_{\max}-1}$.
Thus the series defining $\Psi_B$ converges absolutely on (AQ11),
bounded by $C_B\sum_m(1+|m|)^{-2}$ times its seminorm of order
$d_{\max}+1$. It is continuous and extends the finite sum.

Conversely continuity of a linear map from (AQ11) to finite-dimensional
$E$ bounds it by $C\sup_m(1+|m|)^r\|v_m\|$ for some finite $C,r$:
continuity at zero supplies finitely many seminorms, and the largest
dominates all of them. Test a sequence supported at the single index
$m$ with coefficient an eigenvector $v$. It gives
$|\lambda|^m\|v\|\le C(1+|m|)^r\|v\|$ for all integers $m$.
Letting $m$ tend to positive and negative infinity proves $|\lambda|=1$.
This also proves uniqueness, since truncation converges in every
Schwartz seminorm (use a seminorm of one higher order for the tail).

For (AQ1), the actual eigenvalues in (AQ7) have moduli
$p^{(2a-k)\delta}$. Thus (AQ12) fails at every $\delta>0$ in the
specified family. The exact root functional at $i=(a,b)$ has norm on
the finite word space
\[
 \|\operatorname{ev}_{y_i}\Psi_p\|^2
   =\frac{(D_N)_{ii}}{w_p}
           \sum_{m=-J}^Jp^{2m(2a-k)\delta}.
 \tag{AQ14}
\]
This is both the diagonal case of (AQ8) and a direct finite dual norm
calculation. It gives the complete rate of its failure to have a
polynomial sequence bound; no projected sign or total determinant
has been used to assign that rate.

At the literal collision $\delta=0$, keep the same polynomial rather
than replacing it by its square-free part:
$Q_k(y)=\prod_{b=0}^k(y-(2b-k)\gamma)^{k+1}$.
The Chinese remainder map gives $k+1$ blocks
$\mathbb C[u]/u^{k+1}$ with $M=(2b-k)\gamma+u$ on the $b$th block.
On that block the original centred powers are
\[
 B_p^m=e^{im(2b-k)\gamma\log p}
       \sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{(im\log p)^j}{j!}u^j.
 \tag{AQ15}
\]
This follows from the terminating exponential in the same quotient
algebra. Its growth is at most polynomial of degree $k$, so (AQ12)
holds without deleting any repeated root or nilpotent jet. The exact
formula (AQ6) also remains valid at this collision; only the
distinct-root coordinate formula (AQ8) is replaced by (AQ15).

For every parameter, including the collision, the finite-sequence map
has a receiving space selected by its full matrix:
\[
 \mathscr A_B=\{(v_m):\sum_m\|B^m v_m\|_{G_N}<\infty\},
 \qquad\|(v_m)\|_{\mathscr A_B}=\sum_m\|B^m v_m\|_{G_N}.
 \tag{AQ16}
\]
The coordinatewise map $(v_m)\mapsto(B^m v_m)$ is an isometric
bijection to $\ell^1(\mathbb Z;E)$, so this is a complete normed
space. The sum map extends (AQ3) continuously and surjectively to it.
The sequence shift and its inverse are bounded there, with norms at
most $\|B\|$ and $\|B^{-1}\|$, respectively, and the exact
intertwining relation (AQ4) persists. This constructs the object
defined by the failed Schwartz extension, rather than discarding
the arithmetic quotient. The connecting inclusion of finite
sequences into both (AQ11) and (AQ16), and their common map (AQ3),
are explicit. No off-circle eigenvalue has been removed by declaration.

\subsection{The compact-to-ordinary map on the computed affine base}
For the universal coupling on $Y=S\triangleright\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Z$,
the full recollement proof gives
$R\Gamma(Y,U\mathcal O_X)=R\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)$ and
\[
 R\Gamma_X(Y,U\mathcal O_X)
 \longrightarrow R\Gamma(X,\mathcal O_X)
 \longrightarrow R\Gamma(S,\mathbb Q).
 \tag{AQ17}
\]
The middle complex is $\mathbb Z$ in degree zero and zero in positive
degrees. This can be proved directly here: the exact principal-parts
sequence $0\to\mathcal O_X\to\mathbb Q_X\to
\bigoplus_p i_{p*}(\mathbb Q_p/\mathbb Z_p)\to0$ has flasque
last two terms. Constants on nonempty opens of the irreducible $X$
give surjective restrictions in $\mathbb Q_X$; restriction in the
direct sum of point sheaves is deletion of coordinates. The global
principal-parts map is onto by writing a finite collection of
$p$-power fractions and adding them. Its kernel is $\mathbb Z$.
This computes the asserted cohomology, including all primes.

Consequently the natural relative-to-ordinary map in (AQ17) has
zero image in every positive degree, while its boundary groups are
exactly (FD1) and the higher support groups. In degree zero its
relative source is zero, since $\mathbb Z\to\mathbb Q^c$ is injective.
This calculates the particular image; it does not identify a
Zariski support functor with the compact support functor of an
arithmetic curve containing its Archimedean data. The exact
relation is the triangle (AQ17). The relative boundary is retained
as its cone, with the operators (FD3)--(FD7), and is the source
of the arithmetic quotient (AQ3).

These calculations locate the next purity issue precisely. The full
relative spectrum supplies an actual degree operator and its pure
circle norm identity. It also supplies a distributional quotient onto
the original arithmetic action, whose metric and extension space have
now been evaluated. The Zariski relative-to-ordinary image on the
affine universal coupling is zero; it cannot be substituted for the
unconstructed global image involving the Archimedean term and both
pole maps. Establishing that global map's regularity and dual pairing
requires further mathematics. The statements (AQ12)--(AQ16) specify
the exact objects and growth distinction that such a map must address;
they are complete calculations, not an assumed purity theorem or an RH
conclusion.

\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=13mm,
 every node/.style={align=center},
 box/.style={draw,rounded corners,text width=118mm,inner sep=5pt}]
\node[box] (coh) {$D_S=\mathbb Q^c/\Delta\mathbb Z$\\
 $0\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z\to D_S\to\mathbb Q^c/\Delta\mathbb Q\to0$};
\node[box,below=of coh] (deg) {Every fibre of $d\mapsto pd$ has $p$ elements\\
 $A_{p,S}^*A_{p,S}=pI$; full support retained};
\node[box,below=of deg] (word) {Actual $k$-fold relative operator on prime words\\
 $T_p(h_m\otimes v)=p^{k/2}h_{m+1}\otimes v$};
\node[box,below=of word] (original) {Original quotient $\Psi_p(\sum h_m\otimes v_m)=\sum B_p^mv_m$\\
 $\Psi_pT_p=F_p\Psi_p$; full finite metric (AQ6)--(AQ10)};
\node[box,below=of original] (growth) {Actual root growth $|\alpha_{ab}|=p^{(2a-k)\delta}$\\
 Schwartz extension exactly at unit moduli; repeated jets retained};
\draw[-{Latex}] (coh)--node[right]{(FD3)--(FD4)}(deg);
\draw[-{Latex}] (deg)--node[right]{(PD20), (AQ2)}(word);
\draw[-{Latex}] (word)--node[right]{(AQ3)--(AQ5)}(original);
\draw[-{Latex}] (original)--node[right]{(AQ12)--(AQ16)}(growth);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The exact link from the added prime and full support to the
original arithmetic action. The prime degree fixes its square-root
factor. The arithmetic quotient keeps every original eigenvalue,
coordinate and metric; its growth is calculated rather than assigned.
The diagram does not identify this quotient with Deligne's geometric
image. The latter comparison is (AQ17) and the scope following it;
Deligne's human-source theorem is Weil II, (3.3.6) \cite{DeligneII}.}
\end{figure}

\section{The completed prime-word cohomology and its exact image}
\label{sec:completed-prime-word}
The quotient (AQ3) now permits an actual image calculation, rather
than a proposed regularity assumption. The source is the prime-word
subspace of the relative operator (AQ2), with its physical valuation
index $m\in\mathbb Z$. We compute its finite-support and rapid-decay
cohomology, the map between them, and the full relative object
recording the classes that this map kills. This is a local comparison
built from the split relative-prime operator. Its construction is not
identified with global arithmetic compactly supported cohomology.

\subsection{Three exact coefficient complexes}
Keep $E,G_N,k,p,F_p,B_p$ from (AQ1), with their original factors.
For an arbitrary invertible complex matrix $B$ on $E$, let
\[
 \begin{array}{lll}
 \Phi=E^{(\mathbb Z)},&
 \mathscr S=\mathscr S(\mathbb Z;E),&
 \Pi=E^{\mathbb Z},\\
 (sv)_m=v_{m-1},&& d_B=sI-B.
 \end{array}
 \tag{PC1}
\]
The first is finite-support sequences, the second is exactly (AQ11),
and the third is all sequences, with no decay imposed. The differential
is $(d_Bv)_m=v_{m-1}-Bv_m$ in each case. Put each complex in degrees
zero and one. Its degree action is $p^{k/2}s$ on both terms; this
commutes with $d_B$, so it induces an action on cohomology. For $B=B_p$
this is the actual tensor relative-prime degree in (AQ4).

The finite complex has the exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow\Phi\xrightarrow{d_B}\Phi
 \xrightarrow{\Psi_B}E\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \Psi_B(v)=\sum_mB^mv_m.
 \tag{PC2}
\]
Surjectivity and the kernel equality are (AQ3) and (AQ5). Injectivity
of $d_B$ follows directly: for a nonzero finite sequence, take its
largest nonzero index $m$; then $(d_Bv)_{m+1}=v_m\ne0$.
Consequently its cohomology is $E$ in degree one and zero in degree
zero. The degree action on that cohomology is $p^{k/2}B$ by (AQ4).

At the other endpoint the all-sequence complex has
\[
 H^0(\Pi\xrightarrow{d_B}\Pi)
   =\{(B^{-m}v)_{m\in\mathbb Z}:v\in E\}\simeq E,
 \qquad H^1(\Pi\xrightarrow{d_B}\Pi)=0.
 \tag{PC3}
\]
The homogeneous recurrence is exactly $v_m=B^{-1}v_{m-1}$, proving
the kernel formula in both directions. For a prescribed sequence
$w$, choose $v_0=0$, solve
$v_m=B^{-1}(v_{m-1}-w_m)$ successively for $m\ge1$, and solve
$v_{m-1}=w_m+Bv_m$ successively for $m\le0$. This produces a
two-sided sequence with $d_Bv=w$ and proves surjectivity. The degree
action on its kernel again corresponds to $p^{k/2}B$, since
$(sv)_m=B^{-(m-1)}v=B^{-m}Bv$. Thus the full algebraic receiver
retains every original eigenvalue, in its stated cohomological degree.

\subsection{Rapid sequences and smooth division on the original circle}
The Fourier series map is
\[
 \mathcal F:\mathscr S\xrightarrow{\sim}C^\infty(\mathbb T;E),
 \qquad (\mathcal Fv)(z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb Z}z^mv_m,
 \quad |z|=1.
 \tag{PC4}
\]
For a rapid sequence each differentiated series converges absolutely
and uniformly, since multiplication of its $m$th coefficient by any
power of $m$ still gives an absolutely summable sequence. Conversely
the coefficient formula
$v_m=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi}f(e^{i\theta})e^{-im\theta}\,d\theta$
and integration by parts any number of times give every seminorm in
(AQ11). The Fourier coefficients determine the smooth function: for
example convolution with the finite Fejer kernels, whose coefficients
are $1-|m|/(M+1)$ for $|m|\le M$, tends uniformly to a continuous
periodic function. This follows directly from their nonnegativity,
integral one, and the formula
$(M+1)^{-1}|\sum_{j=0}^Me^{ij\theta}|^2$, which tends uniformly to
zero outside any neighbourhood of zero. Thus zero coefficients force
the function to be zero, and the two displayed operations are inverse.
These estimates also prove continuity in both smooth and sequence
seminorms. In these exact coordinates, $d_B$ is multiplication by
$zI-B$.

Let $E_\lambda$ be the generalized eigenspace of $B$ at $\lambda$,
$P_\lambda$ its spectral projection, and
$N_\lambda=(B-\lambda I)|_{E_\lambda}$ with nilpotence index
$d_\lambda$. The decomposition and its projections are algebraic:
Bezout's identity for the pairwise coprime powers
$(t-\lambda)^{d_\lambda}$ gives their Chinese remainder idempotents,
evaluated at $B$. This proves the direct-sum decomposition and does
not require semisimplicity or distinct eigenvalues. Put
\[
 E_\circ=\bigoplus_{|\lambda|=1}E_\lambda,
 \quad E_{\rm off}=\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\ne1}E_\lambda,
 \quad P_\circ=\sum_{|\lambda|=1}P_\lambda.
 \tag{PC5}
\]
For $z=e^{i\theta}$, write $\partial_z=(iz)^{-1}\partial_\theta$.
This is a specified complex differential operator on smooth functions
on the circle; it is not an assumption of a holomorphic extension.
Define the complete jet receiver
\[
 \mathcal J_B(f)=
 \sum_{|\lambda|=1}\sum_{j=0}^{d_\lambda-1}
   \frac{N_\lambda^j}{j!}
   (\partial_z^j P_\lambda f)(\lambda)\ \in E_\circ.
 \tag{PC6}
\]
The sum of finitely many derivative evaluations is continuous. For
a constant function $f=v\in E_\circ$, it has value $v$, so it is
surjective.

\begin{theorem}[The entire rapid-decay cohomology]
\label{thm:rapid-prime-cohomology}
There is an exact sequence of complex vector spaces and continuous maps
\[
 0\longrightarrow C^\infty(\mathbb T;E)
 \xrightarrow{zI-B} C^\infty(\mathbb T;E)
 \xrightarrow{\mathcal J_B}E_\circ\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{PC7}
\]
The first map has closed range. Consequently the complex on
$\mathscr S$ in (PC1) has $H^0=0$, $H^1=E_\circ$; no off-circle
eigenspace or unit-circle Jordan block is omitted from this assertion.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Away from the finitely many unit-circle eigenvalues, $zI-B$ is
invertible. A smooth function in its kernel vanishes on that dense
open set and hence everywhere, proving injectivity.

On a primary component with $|\lambda|\ne1$, the inverse
$\sum_{j=0}^{d_\lambda-1}(z-\lambda)^{-j-1}N_\lambda^j$
is smooth on the entire circle. There is therefore no cokernel
contribution from that component.

For $|\lambda|=1$, a smooth component $f_\lambda$ has a local finite
expansion
\[
 f_\lambda(z)=\sum_{r=0}^{d_\lambda-1}a_r(z-\lambda)^r
           +(z-\lambda)^{d_\lambda}g(z),
 \quad a_r=\frac{\partial_z^rf_\lambda(\lambda)}{r!},
 \tag{PC8}
\]
with $g$ smooth. To justify this for smooth, possibly nonholomorphic
data, use a real coordinate $t=\theta-\theta_0$. The quotient
$(z(t)-\lambda)/t$ is smooth and nonzero at zero. The fundamental
theorem of calculus gives a smooth quotient by $t$ for every smooth
function vanishing at zero. Repeating this division gives the finite
expansion; applying $\partial_z$ successively identifies its
coefficients as the displayed derivatives.

Multiplication of (PC8) by the finite inverse above has, at the
negative power $(z-\lambda)^{-l}$, exactly the coefficient
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{d_\lambda-l}N_\lambda^{r+l-1}a_r
  =N_\lambda^{l-1}\sum_{r=0}^{d_\lambda-1}N_\lambda^r a_r,
 \qquad 1\le l\le d_\lambda.
 \tag{PC9}
\]
Terms added on the right have powers at least $d_\lambda$ and are
zero. The coefficient for $l=1$ is precisely the $\lambda$ component
of (PC6); all other negative coefficients are its displayed multiples.
Thus if (PC6) vanishes, the inverse applied to $f_\lambda$ has a
smooth extension at $\lambda$. It is smooth elsewhere already and
solves $(zI-B)g=f$ on the whole circle. Conversely, if $f=(zI-B)g$
with smooth $g$, its inverse away from the point is that smooth
function. A nonzero highest negative Laurent power in (PC9) is
unbounded in some linear coordinate as $t\to0$; hence all negative
coefficients must vanish, including $l=1$. This proves that the
kernel of (PC6) is exactly the range. Surjectivity was proved after
(PC6), completing exactness. The range is closed as the kernel of a
continuous map to a finite-dimensional space.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The comparison image and every lost class}
For a finite Laurent polynomial $f(z)=\sum z^mv_m$, the terminating
Taylor formula on each primary block, including negative powers,
gives
\[
 \mathcal J_B(f)=P_\circ\sum_mB^mv_m=P_\circ\Psi_B(v).
 \tag{PC10}
\]
For negative powers this is exactly the inverse-block binomial
calculation (AQ13). Hence the inclusion of finite sequences into
rapid sequences induces the fully evaluated cohomology map
\[
 \boxed{H^1(\Phi\xrightarrow{d_B}\Phi)=E
       \xrightarrow{\ P_\circ\ }
       H^1(\mathscr S\xrightarrow{d_B}\mathscr S)=E_\circ.}
 \tag{PC11}
\]
Its image is the entire right side, its kernel is $E_{\rm off}$, and
its cokernel is zero. Its degree action is the actual
$p^{k/2}B|_{E_\circ}$. Every eigenvalue on the image has absolute
value $p^{k/2}$. This proves a local purity-circle image statement for
these specified complexes. It does not assert the arithmetic
integrality of these eigenvalues or Deligne's purity at every
embedding of an algebraic coefficient field.

The relative object of this completion is also completely determined.
Let $C_\Phi,C_{\mathscr S}$ denote the two complexes in (PC1).
The homotopy fibre of their actual inclusion has
\[
 H^1\bigl(\operatorname{Cone}(C_\Phi\to C_{\mathscr S})[-1]\bigr)
       =E_{\rm off},\qquad H^j=0\quad(j\ne1).
 \tag{PC12}
\]
Indeed the long exact sequence of the explicit cone uses the
cohomology already proved, and its only nonzero portion is
$0\to H^1\to E\xrightarrow{P_\circ}E_\circ\to H^2\to0$.
The last map is surjective, so the assertion follows with its exact
kernel. This retains the removed classes as a derived object with
their unchanged original degree action $p^{k/2}B|_{E_{\rm off}}$.
The finite, rapid, and all-sequence constructions (PC2), (PC7), and
(PC3) therefore specify both which receiver retains each class and
its cohomological degree. No class is declared nonexistent merely
because one completion does not receive it.

For the actual arithmetic family (AQ1) with $\delta>0$, odd $k$
implies $2a-k\ne0$ for every original root. Thus (AQ7) gives
\[
 E_\circ=0,\qquad E_{\rm off}=E,
 \quad H^1(C_{\mathscr S})=0,
 \quad H^1\bigl(\operatorname{Cone}(C_\Phi\to C_{\mathscr S})[-1]\bigr)=E.
 \tag{PC13}
\]
This is a calculation for the displayed hypothetical off-line family.
The finite source and the all-sequence kernel still contain all $q$
dimensions and their original phases. It is not a contradiction
unless another proved geometric map requires a nonzero such class to
survive precisely the comparison (PC11).

At $\delta=0$, keep every repeated root and nilpotent coefficient of
(AQ15). All eigenvalues of $B_p$ have modulus one, including any
coincident phases at the selected prime. Hence $E_\circ=E$, and
(PC11) is the identity on the original $q$-dimensional coefficient
algebra. The receiver (PC6) retains derivatives through order $k$
on the size-$k+1$ blocks of (AQ15). When different blocks have the
same exponential eigenvalue, their direct sum is the primary space
used in (PC6); no block is merged into a single vector or discarded.
The full relative object (PC12) is then acyclic.

\subsection{What has been supplied for the global purity problem}
The construction uses the additional arithmetic prime through its
relative principal parts, the actual $p$-to-one multiplication, and
the resulting $k$-fold degree action. Its comparison is now a proved
map with a computed image, kernel, and full collision specialization.
The point at which Deligne's method would require additional global
information is explicit: a geometric compact-to-ordinary map must
be related to this local completion by actual chain maps, retaining
the two pole classes, the Archimedean contribution, and the original
observation and conductor maps. The affine comparison (AQ17), whose
image was calculated as zero, is not substituted for those maps.
Equations (PC11)--(PC13) prevent a false shortcut: choosing a
completion that keeps only a purity circle proves the stated image
calculation, but does not by itself prove that a class from the
original global trace has to survive it. The exact relative object
where such a class can reside is (PC12), not an unrecorded error term.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
0\arrow[r]&\Phi\arrow[r,"sI-B"]\arrow[d,hook]&
 \Phi\arrow[r,"\Psi_B"]\arrow[d,hook]&E\arrow[r]\arrow[d,"P_\circ"]&0\\
0\arrow[r]&\mathscr S\arrow[r,"sI-B"]&
 \mathscr S\arrow[r,"\mathcal J_B\mathcal F"]&E_\circ\arrow[r]&0
\end{tikzcd}
\caption{The exact local cohomology comparison (PC2), (PC7), and
(PC10). The upper terms are finite prime-word sequences in the
relative-degree receiver; the lower terms retain every rapid sequence.
The right-hand kernel is exactly $E_{\rm off}$, retained by (PC12).
Both rows carry the unchanged degree action $p^{k/2}s$, inducing
$p^{k/2}B$ on their images. This is a computed local comparison,
with the global Deligne comparison and its human-source theorem
specified in the text.}
\end{figure}

\section{The exact spectrum of support operations and transfers}


\subsection{The full support object and the endomorphism to be computed}

Let $L$ be a finite nontrivial bounded distributive lattice,
$S=\operatorname{SpecLat}L$, $X=\Spec\Z$, and
$Y=S\triangleright X$. The opens of $Y$ are the opens of $S$ and
the sets $S\cup U$ with nonempty open $U\subset X$. This is the
entire spectrum of the original semiring
\[
 \G_L(\Z)=\{(0,\lambda):\lambda\in L\}
       \cup\{(a,1_L):a\in\Z\}.
 \tag{SE1}\label{eq:original-space}
\]
The supported zero $e=(0,1_L)$ and the additive identity
